(106)

The detailed computations of these diagrams are again summarised in Section
C.2

and the final result is simply
18
18

18

This form is expected from gauge invariance and supersymmetry. In fact, if one considers separately the fermionic and bosonic loops, they contain (with opposite signs) additional term which are not proportional to the scalar propagator, see Appendix
C.2
.

{D}^{(1),MN}_{ab}(X_{12})=\delta^{MN}{D}_{ab}^{(1)}(X_{12})\ .

(107)

It is now extremely straightforward to compute the dimensionally regularized correction to the vacuum expectation value of the Wilson loop given by (
102
). Employing (
107
) and (
103
) and taking the colour taccording (
73
), we find that

{g}_{B}^{4}\Delta\mathcal{W}_{4}=-4\pi^{2}\beta_{0}^{\mathcal{R}}{{g}_{B}^{4}C_{F}}\oint\differential^{2}s\big[{R}^{2}-\dot{X}_{1}\cdot\dot{X}_{2}\big]I(X_{12})\ .\\

(108)

This expression vanishes in superconformal set-ups as it is proportional to the one-loop coefficient
\beta^{\mathcal{R}}_{0}
of the
\beta
-function defined in (
38
). Using the explicit expression of the massless integral
I(X_{12})
, given by (
104
), and the parametrization (
96
) we get

\Delta\mathcal{W}_{4}=\Delta\mathcal{W}^{\rm flat}_{4}+\delta\mathcal{W}_{4}\ ,

(109)

where the additional term w.r.t. flat space

\delta\mathcal{W}_{4}=(4-d)\dfrac{C_{F}\beta^{\mathcal{R}}_{0}\Gamma^{2}(d/2-1)}{16\pi^{d-2}(d-3)}\oint\differential^{2}s\dfrac{{R}^{2}-\dot{X}_{1}\cdot\dot{X}_{2}}{|X_{12}|^{2d-6}}F(X_{12})+\ldots\

(110)

vanishes for
d\to 4
and will only contribute at higher orders in perturbation theory.

Finally, the ladder-like contribution at order
g_{B}^{4}
, namely
\Sigma_{\mathrm{ladder}}^{S}
in (
100
), takes the same form of the flat space one:

\Sigma_{\mathrm{ladder}}=\Sigma_{\mathrm{ladder}}^{S}\ ,

(111)

as one can verify with a straightforward calculation employing the propagators of the gauge field and of the adjoint scalar, i.e. (
158
) and (
157
).

Altogether, the corrections of order
g_{B}^{4}
we determined on the sphere differ from those in flat space only by evanescent terms:

\mathcal{W}_{4}=\mathcal{W}_{4}^{\rm flat}+\mathcal{O}(d-4)~.

(112)

Since at the one-loop level we found that
\mathcal{W}_{2}=\mathcal{W}_{2}^{\rm flat}
, it follows that the dimensionally regularized observable on the sphere (
97
) and that computed in flat space (
63
) satisfy

\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}^{\rm flat}+\ldots\ ,

(113)

where the dots stand for terms of order
g_{B}^{4}
that vanish for
d\to 4
and terms of order
g_{B}^{6}
and higher.

5
Renormalization

The two-loop corrections
\mathcal{W}_{4}^{\rm flat}
and
\mathcal{W}_{4}
to the vacuum expectation value of the supersymmetric Wilson loop operator in flat space and on the sphere are divergent for
d\to 4
since the
\Delta\mathcal{W}_{4}^{\rm flat}
and
\Delta\mathcal{W}_{4}
contributions,
given by (
77
) and (
109
) respectively, contain simple poles
1/(4-d)
.

These ultraviolet singularities are the only divergences affecting the regularized observables in the limit
d\to 4
. To obtain a finite result we have to apply the renormalization procedure. For smooth curves, such as the contours on which we placed the 1/2 BPS Wilson loop in flat space (
62
) and on the sphere (
96
), the UV singularities are reabsorbed just by the charge renormalization
Z_{g}
, i.e. by setting

Korchemsky:1987wg

\begin{split}g_{B}&=Z_{g}(d)g_{*}(\mu)\mu^{2-d/2}\\
\end{split}

(114)

where
\mu
is the renormalization scale and
g_{*}(\mu)
is the renormalized coupling. The ultraviolet divergent contributions in
Z_{g}
are obtained by usual subtraction operations and are the same in flat space and on the sphere
Drummond:1975yc
, since in the short-distance limit the two spaces are undistinguishable. Expressing the dimensionally regularized observables in terms of the renormalized coupling via eq. (
114
), all the divergences are removed and we can take the limit
d\to 4
, defining

\begin{split}W^{\rm flat}&=\lim_{d\to 4}\mathcal{W}^{\rm flat}~.\end{split}

(115)

Analogously, we define
W
as the limit
d\to 4
of
\mathcal{W}
on the sphere.
Let us note that
W^{\rm flat}
, as well as
W
, can depend on
\mu
via
g_{*}(\mu)
and on the dimensionless quantity
\mu R
. The overall dependence on
\mu
is unphysical and consequently, the following Callan-Symanzik equation

\displaystyle\Big(\beta(g_{*})\dfrac{\partial}{\partial g_{*}}+R\dfrac{\partial}{\partial R}\Big){W}^{\rm flat}=0

(116)

must hold.
Here
\beta(g_{*})=\mu\partial_{\mu}g_{*}
is the
\beta
-function of the theory. This CS equation implies that
W^{\rm flat}
is expressible as a function of the running coupling
g(R)
, which satisfies the evolution equation

\dfrac{\differential{g}}{\differential\log\mu R}=\beta(g)

(117)

and determines its value at the energy scale
1/R
in terms of the initial condition
g(\mu)=g_{*}(\mu)
. Thus, we must have

{W}^{\rm flat}={W}^{\rm flat}(g(R))~,

(118)

and similarly for the sphere quantity
W(g(R))
. Recalling that the (one-loop exact)
\beta
-function of a
\mathcal{N}=2
SYM theory with hypermultiplets in the representation
\mathcal{R}
is
\beta(g)=g^{3}\beta_{0}^{\mathcal{R}}
, with the coefficient
\beta_{0}^{\mathcal{R}}
defined in (
38
), we can easily integrate (
117
) to obtain

g^{2}(R)=\dfrac{g_{*}^{2}}{1+2\beta_{0}^{\mathcal{R}}g_{*}^{2}\ln(\mu R)}\ .

(119)

Let us observe that, if we identity the renormalization scale
\mu
with the mass scale
M
we introduced in the localization approach, this equation coincides with the coupling (
58
) appearing in the regularized matrix model associated with these theories.

5.1
Results to order
g_{*}^{4}

We now derive the perturbative expansion, up to order
g_{*}^{4}
, of the renormalized observables
{W}^{\rm flat}
and
{W}
, which we will compare with the localization prediction (
60
). We will begin with deriving the results in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
.

Standard computations in flat space show that, to order
g_{*}^{2}
, the charge renormalization is given by

{Z}_{g}(d)=1+\beta_{0}^{\mathcal{R}}g_{*}^{2}\left(\dfrac{1}{{4-d}}+K_{1}\right)+\mathcal{O}(g_{*}^{4})\ ,

(120)

where
K_{1}
characterizes the renormalization scheme.
Using this expression in eq. (
114
) we can express the results of Section
3
in terms of
g_{*}
.
For the lowest-order correction,
given by (
66
), we have

\begin{split}g_{B}^{2}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{\rm flat}\ \to\ \dfrac{C_{F}}{4}g_{*}^{2}+\dfrac{\beta^{\mathcal{R}}_{0}g_{*}^{4}C_{F}}{2(4-d)}+g_{*}^{4}\beta^{\mathcal{R}}_{0}\dfrac{C_{F}}{4}\Big(\gamma_{E}+2K_{1}+\log{\mu^2R^2\pi}\Big)+\mathcal{O}(g_{*}^{6})\ .\end{split}

(121)

Analogously, the divergent contribution at two-loop accuracy,
defined in (
77
), yields

{g}_{B}^{4}\Delta\mathcal{W}^{\rm flat}_{4}\ \to\ -\dfrac{C_{F}g_{*}^{4}\beta^{\mathcal{R}}_{0}}{2(4-d)}-\dfrac{C_{F}g_{*}^{4}\beta^{\mathcal{R}}_{0}}{2}\Big(1+\gamma_{E}+\log{\pi R^2\mu^2}\Big)+\mathcal{O}(g_{*}^{6})\ .

(122)

Combining together eq. (
121
) with eq. (
122
), we find that the UV divergences cancel each other out, leaving a well-defined quantity at order
g_{*}^{4}
as expected.
Thus, we can take the limit
d\to 4
and find that the final expression for the renormalized observable at order
{g}_{*}^{4}
is

W^{\rm flat}=\dfrac{C_{F}g_{*}^{2}}{4}+\dfrac{C_{F}g_{*}^{4}}{192N}(2N^{2}-3)-\dfrac{C_{F}g_{*}^{4}}{4}\beta^{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(2+\gamma_{E}-2K_{1}+\log\mu^{2}{R}^{2}\pi)+\mathcal{O}(g_{*}^{6})\ ,

(123)

where we also included the ladder contribution (
89
), proportional to
g_{*}^{4}
.

The analysis on the sphere is analogous. Let us begin with observing that, up to order
g_{B}^{4}
, the dimensionally regularized observable
\mathcal{W}
defined on the sphere coincides with
\mathcal{W}^{\rm flat}
, except for terms that vanish for
d\to 4
(see eq. (
113
)). As a result, the renormalization procedure gives us again (
123
). Indeed, when rewriting
g_{B}
in terms of the renormalized coupling
g_{*}
via eq. (
114
), at order
g_{*}^{4}
we only have to replace
Z_{g}\sim 1
. Taking the limit
d\to 4
, the additional contributions in (
113
) proportional to
d-4
vanish and we have

W=W^{\rm flat}+\mathcal{O}(g_{*}^{6})\ .

(124)

Our final task is to compare the renormalized observables with the localization prediction (
60
). In particular, using (
2.3
) and (
58
), we can cast the matrix model prediction as follows

W(g_{*})=\dfrac{g_{*}^{2}C_{F}}{4}+\dfrac{g_{*}^{4}C_{F}}{192N}(2N^{2}-3)-g_{*}^{4}\frac{\beta_{0}^{\mathcal{R}}\ C_{F}}{2}\log MR+\mathcal{O}(g_{*}^{6})~.

(125)

Setting
\mu=M
we note that
both
the renormalized observable on the sphere and in flat space, i.e. (
124
) and (
123
), perfectly matches the previous expression if we fix the renormalization scheme as follows

K_{1}=\dfrac{2+\gamma_{E}+\log\pi}{2}\ .

(126)

It is interesting to note that the choice of the renormalization scheme is identical in flat space and on the sphere. This stems from the fact that, at this perturbative order, the finite parts resulting from the renormalization procedure are identical
even if the diagrams are defined on spaces connected by conformal symmetry – which is broken at the quantum level.
In the following section, we will discuss further effects resulting from the renormalization procedure which, starting from order
g_{*}^{6}
, make the analysis in these models even more subtle.

5.2
Observations regarding higher perturbative orders

The replacement of the bare coupling constant
g_{B}
via (
114
) has to be applied to all the contributions appearing in the dimensionally regularized observables. As a result, evanescent corrections, i.e. terms proportional to
(d-4)
, are activated by the poles of the charge renormalization
Z_{g}
and contribute to the renormalized observables at higher orders in perturbation theory. Through out this work we encountered different evanescent contributions on the four-sphere and in flat space. Since they have a different origin, we discuss them separately.

An interesting effect in flat space

In Section
3.2
, we showed that the regularized v.e.v.
\mathcal{W}
is given, to order
g_{B}^{4}
, by eq. (
90
) and contains the evanescent contributions
\Sigma_{\mathrm{Ev}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{Ev}}^{\prime}

originating from the integration over the Wilson loop contour in the
\Sigma_{2}
and
\Sigma_{\rm ladder}
diagrams.

Once the bare coupling
g_{B}
is expressed in terms of the renormalized one via (
114
), the
(d-4)
factor in
the evanescent contributions is compensated by the simple pole in
Z_{g}
and we obtain finite corrections of order
g_{*}^{6}
. For instance, in Section
3.2
we gave the explicit integral expression for the contribution
\Sigma_{\mathrm{Ev}}
. In this case, the renormalization procedure yields the following
g_{*}^{6}
term:

\begin{split}\dfrac{g_{*}^{6}\beta_{0}^{\mathcal{R}}C_{F}N}{2^{6}\pi^{4}}\int_{0}^{1}\differential\alpha\differential\beta\differential\gamma\delta(1-\alpha-\beta-\gamma)\ \oint\differential^{3}{s}\ \epsilon(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\ \dfrac{\sin s_{13}}{Q}+\ldots\ .\end{split}

(127)

The integral appearing in the previous expression is finite
Erickson:2000af
and the entire correction vanishes in superconformal models.
Moreover, this term, as well as the analogous one coming from
\Sigma_{\mathrm{Ev}}^{\prime}
, is not captured by the difference method we described in Section
3
, since it does not arise from
\Delta\mathcal{W}_{4}^{\rm flat}
. In this work, where we restrict ourselves to study the observable at two-loop accuracy, i.e. at order
g_{*}^{4}
, we can ignore these effects. However, at the next perturbative order, such corrections might play a non-trivial role precisely because they do not stem from the difference theory diagrams, which, naïvely, are the only ones suggested by the matrix model description. Yet, the matrix model prediction at order
g^{6}
, see
19
19

19

Actually, eq. (
57
), describes the three-loop prediction of the matrix model in
\mathcal{N}=2
SQCD with
N_{f}
massless fundamental flavour. In this theory
\mathcal{R}=N_{f}\Box
and
\beta_{0}^{\mathcal{R}}=(N_{f}-2N)/16\pi^{2}
. With these identifications it is straightforward to see that the colour structure of (
127
) coincides with that appearing in the third term on the right-hand side of (
57
) characterized by the
\zeta(3)
-Riemann function.
eq. (
57
), contains a term proportional to
\zeta(3)
characterized by the same colour structure of (
127
).
At this stage of the analysis, is not possible to conclude whether corrections such as that appearing in eq. (
127
) are ultimately captured by the matrix model without computing all contributions at order
g^{6}
. The above observations show in any case that
the relation between perturbation theory in flat space and the matrix model in non-conformal models is not obvious and extremely subtle.

The four-sphere

The two-loop correction
g_{B}^{4}\mathcal{W}_{4}
to the dimensionally regularized observable on the sphere was computed in Section
4.2
by the difference theory approach, which enabled us to extract the divergent part of the spider-like diagram
\Sigma^{S}_{2}
in eq. (
100
). Nevertheless, employing this method we did not obtain the explicit expression of evanescent term
\Sigma_{\rm Ev}^{S}
of eq. (
101
) which, along with the evanescent correction resulting from the ladder-like diagram
\Sigma_{\mathrm{ladder}}^{S}
in eq. (
111
), are expected to produce at order
g_{*}^{6}
similar effects to those we described in flat space.

Let us now consider the further evanescent term
\delta\mathcal{W}_{4}
defined in eq. (
110
). This correction results from the structure of the virtual loop on the sphere and is not present in flat space. After replacing the bare coupling constant
g_{B}
in terms of the renormalized one via (
114
), we generate the following contribution

\begin{split}g_{*}^{6}\dfrac{C_{F}(\beta^{\mathcal{R}}_{0})^{2}}{2}(2\zeta(2)+\log^{2}2-\log 2+1)\ .\end{split}

(128)

To integrate over the Wilson loop contour the function
F(X_{12})
, defined in (
105
), we employed the trigonometric integrals outlined in Appendix
D
.
The role played by the
g_{*}^{6}
correction (
128
) is distinctly different from that originating from the evanescent terms such as that in eq. (
127
). In fact, the contribution appearing in (
128
) is quadratic in
\beta_{0}^{\mathcal{R}}
rather than linear.
It will thus combine with the other finite parts
20
20

20

Let us note that these additional finite parts resulting from the renormalization procedure will contribute with
\log^{2}\mu R
to the renormalized observable.
arising from the renormalization of the divergences proportional to
(\beta_{0}^{\mathcal{R}})^{2}
. Consequently,
these finite parts on the sphere will differ from those in flat space. As a result, the renormalization scheme we will have to employ to match the result on the sphere to the matrix model will be different from the one needed in flat space.

6
Conclusions and future perspectives

In this work, we considered the perturbative computation of the circular 1/2 BPS supersymmetric Wilson loop in
SU(
N
)
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories with massless matter in a generic representation
\mathcal{R}
of the gauge group and thus with a non-vanishing
\beta
-function.
Our aim was to compare the Feynman diagram results, derived both in the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{4}
and on the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{4}
, with the expressions obtained by Pestun’s matrix model generated via supersymmetric localization.

With non-zero
\beta
function the localization approach requires a regularization and the net effect is that the resulting matrix model is expressd in terms of a running coupling, which can be identified with the running coupling constant usually introduced in the field-theoretic description.

Our analysis shows that at quartic order in the renormalized coupling
g_{*}
, by selecting a suitable renormalization scheme, the matrix model prediction matches precisely
the perturbative results both on the four sphere and on flat space, which we computed in dimensional regularization.
Thus at order
g_{*}^{4}
, despite conformal symmetry being broken at the quantum level, placing the
\mathcal{N}=2
theory on the four sphere or on flat space yields an equivalent outcome.

We also point out
that in theories with non-vanishing
\beta
-function the approach based on the difference theory, strongly suggested by the matrix model technique, effectively simplifies the analysis of diagrams with internal vertices but does not account for certain “evanescent” contributions which vanishes when
d\to 4
. In superconformal set-ups, these terms can be safely ignored since the bare coupling does not
experience divergent subtractions. In general, however, they become relevant at
higher orders in perturbation theory since the renormalization procedure makes them finite. In other words, our analysis seems to suggest that
the difference theory method can be safely employed to simplify a specific perturbative order, provided that the evanescent
contributions generated at lower orders are correctly considered.

In a follow-up paper, our goal will be to present an explicit comparison between the two approaches at order
g_{*}^{6}
, further clarifying what information we can extract from the matrix model approach about perturbation theory in flat space and exploring in more details the role of the evanescent terms. It could be also interesting to study the role of the evanescent corrections for the correlators of chiral/antichiral local operators, following the approach pioneered in
Billo:2019job
. More ambitiously, one could try to go beyond the perturbative sector and discuss the relation between the instanton contributions derived from localization and the theory in flat-space. This involves a decompactification limit that has been proved extremely subtle in
{\cal N}=2^{*}
